Platts Oilgram Price Report
Volume 104 / Issue 191 / October 6, 2026

News
OIL FUTURES: Crude rises amid improved Middle East flows, persistent risks
Saudi pipeline restores 5.8 million b/d flow Hormuz traffic drops amid security incidents
US EIA raises Q4 crude price outlook
Crude prices finished a volatile session Oct. 6, as markets weighed improved Middle East energy flows against persistent security threats.
NYMEX November WTI settled up 1 cent/barrel at $89.44/b and ICE December Brent climbed 26 cents/b to $100.58/b.
"The crude oil market is in a battle between increasingly normalized flows via the broad Middle East region alongside additional crude and SPR releases, while on the other side tanker attacks and Houthi escalations remain prevalent," TD Securities analysts Ryan McKay and Bart Melek said in an Oct. 6 note.
Saudi Arabia's East-West pipeline returned to operations within "five or six days" after it was attacked and is pumping 5.8 million barrels/day of crude as of Oct. 6, according to Saudi energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman, Saudi news outlet Al Arabiya reported the same day.
Saudi Arabia's East-West pipeline, with a capacity to transport up to 7 million b/d of crude oil shut following Sept. 10 drone attacks, causing significant disruptions to oil supply from the Red Sea while flows from the Persian Gulf remain troubled.
Meanwhile, ship traffic through the Strait of Hormuz dipped to 22 crossings Oct. 5, from 26 on Oct. 4, as two incidents occurred around the chokepoint that day, S&P Global Commodities at Sea said in a report Oct. 6.

Market analysis
International Crude
East-West oil pipeline now flowing at 5.8 million b/d after restart: Saudi energy minister
East-West pipeline restarted days after drone attack
Pipeline flows at 5.8 million b/d on Oct. 6
Majority of Yanbu loadings not visible since Houthi threat
Saudi Arabia's East-West pipeline returned to operations within "five or six days" after it was attacked and is pumping 5.8 million barrels/day of crude as of Oct. 6, according to Saudi energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman, Saudi news outlet Al Arabiya reported the same day.
Saudi Arabia's East-West pipeline, with a capacity to transport up to 7 million b/d of crude oil, was shut down following Sept. 10 drone attacks, causing significant disruptions to oil supply from the Red Sea while flows from the Persian Gulf remain troubled.
"Not everyone knows that within five or six days [of the attack on the East-West pipeline] we started using the line," the Saudi energy minister told the Made in GCC conference in Bahrain Oct. 6, in comments broadcast on Al Arabiya.
The East-West pipeline is central to Saudi Arabia's contingency plans to bypass shipping disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz amid the US-Iran conflict, allowing the country to continue exporting crude worldwide amid the ongoing conflict in the Middle East.
"And today, this morning, we are back to 5.8 million b/d," Prince Abdulaziz said, noting Saudi Arabia's localization efforts -- ensuring resources for repairs are available in-country -- as the driver for the pipeline's restart within less than a week.
The Saudi energy ministry declined to comment. The East-West pipeline moves Saudi export barrels that are largely priced against Middle East sour crude benchmarks. Platts Dubai, the regional oil price reference for medium-sour crude, was assessed by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, $105.69/b on Oct. 6, down from a recent high of $128/b on Sept. 16. Saudi crude loadings from Yanbu resumed on Sept. 23 after being suspended on Sept. 12 following the attack on the EastWest pipeline, according to ship tracker data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. In the seven months since the war began, Yanbu loadings have averaged 2.8 million b/d, from June's 4 million b/d wartime high to 1.9 million b/d of visible shipments over September, CAS showed on Oct. 6.
-- Lulwa Albuainain

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 6, 2026

Angola's Hungo crude trades in Platts MOC at $19.60/b discount to Dated Brent
Eni sells Hungo cargo to ExxonMobil in Platts MOC
Assessment at all-time low since launch in Jan 2006
Traders blame record freight costs for plunging differentials
A cargo of Hungo crude from Angola traded in the Platts Market on Close assessment process on Oct. 6 at a $19.60/barrel discount to benchmark Dated Brent, representing an all-time low for the grade's cash differential.
In the day's process, Eni offered a Nov. 19-20 loading, 950,000-barrel cargo of the grade from a $12.95/b discount down to a $19.60/b discount which was then bought by ExxonMobil. The last time Hungo traded in the Platts MOC was almost one year earlier on Oct. 15, 2025, when Eni again sold to PetroIneos at a $2.95/b discount for a November 2025 cargo.
The Oct. 6 trade occurred in the third pricing session that Eni offered the Nov. 19-20 loading clip, following the Oct. 1 and Oct. 2 sessions. In the first session, Eni opened their offer at a $1/b discount to Dated Brent, representing an $18.60/b softening across the three days.
Platts assessed Hungo at a $19.60/b discount to Dated Brent on Oct. 6, the lowest ever assessment for the grade since its launch by Platts in January 2006.
The monumental fall in cash differentials for grades like Angola's Hungo has been blamed by West African crude traders on unprecedented freight costs which have made shifting crude on long haul voyages very difficult.
The cargo traded in the day's Platts MOC is the only cargo of Hungo scheduled to load in November, according to a copy of the grade's loading schedule seen by Platts.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- George Delaney
IRAQ DATA: Sep crude exports rise 25% on month as Hormuz flows rebound
Exports reach nearly 3 million b/d
Gulf terminal shipments up 321,000 b/d on month
Launched exports via Syrian port of Banias
Iraqi crude exports rose 25% month over month in September, as more Iraqi oil passed through the Strait of Hormuz, according to data from state oil marketer SOMO, obtained exclusively by Platts Oct. 5.
The data showed that exports hit 2.960 million barrels/day in September, up 583,000 b/d from August levels.
Despite the substantial increase, September flows were around 83% of the 3.568 million b/d Iraq exported in February, before shipping restrictions and attacks linked to the US-Iran war disrupted production and exports in the Middle East.
September exports included 2.571 million b/d from the

southern Gulf terminals, up 321,000 b/d month over month. This was up from 2.250 million b/d in August, but is well below the February rate of 3.335 million b/d.
The rise in exports from the Gulf terminals was due to the increased number of tankers carrying Iraqi oil arriving and loading after crossing the Strait of Hormuz. On Oct. 4, Iraqi Oil Minister Basim Mohammed Khudair al-Abadi said "Iraq had endured difficult circumstances in the recent past, but the crisis is beginning to subside," according to an oil ministry statement.
Gulf terminal exports accounted for 70% of the oil passing through the Strait of Hormuz during the month, SOMO chief Ali Shatri said Sept. 3.
September Basrah Gulf terminal exports consisted of 1.968 million b/d of Basrah Medium crude and 603,000 b/d of Basrah Heavy crude. This compares to 1.799 million b/d and 451,000 b/d in August, respectively, the SOMO data showed. Exports remain below February levels of 2.120 million b/d of Basrah Medium crude and 1.215 million b/d of Basrah Heavy crude.
Basrah Medium has a nominal API gravity of 29 degrees, while Basrah Heavy carries a gravity of 23-24 API.
Iraq also increased exports via Ceyhan and the Syrian port of Banias and resumed supplies to Qayara, the data showed.
Ceyhan exports, which consisted of oil from Kirkuk and the Kurdistan region, doubled month over month to 254,000 b/d.
These volumes, pumped via the Kurdistan pipeline, increased from 180,000 b/d in July and 198,000 b/d in February.
This indicates that IOCs in Kurdistan resumed exports of around 120,000 b/d during the month. This resumption was due to attacks on the Kurdistan region ceasing in early August.
In August, all exports from Ceyhan were Kirkuk federal oil pumped through the Kurdistan pipeline, and no oil was supplied by the IOCs working in Kurdistan.
Exports are below the 250,000 b/d that the Kurdistan Regional Government agreed to pump through their pipeline in mid-March. Under that agreement, some 170,000-200,000 b/d of crude from Basrah was to be pumped North, as volumes produced from Kirkuk are not sufficient to meet export and local refinery demand. In August, the fall was attributed by Shatri to shortages of road trucks to transport the oil from Basrah to the northern system.
Exports of heavy Qayara oil resumed in September at a rate of 56,000 b/d. They had been suspended since February, when the export rate was 35,000 b/d. Trucking to Jordan has remained suspended since January.
In its efforts to diversify export routes and increase export volumes, the oil ministry started supplying oil by trucks from Basrah through Syria for export from the port of Banias on the Mediterranean. These supplies averaged 85,000 b/d in September.
Iraqi government oil revenues were hit by a $25-$35/b discount on the OSP to compensate for risks to tankers passing through the Strait of Hormuz .
-- Faleh Al-Khayat

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 6, 2026

ICE Dubai crude futures traded volume edges down MOM in September
Traded volume at 2.427 mil lots in September
Middle East crude supply volatile in September
The volume of ICE Dubai crude futures traded on the Intercontinental Exchange edged 0.1% lower month over month to 2.427 million lots in September, exchange data showed Oct. 6.
ICE Dubai crude futures comprise Dubai first-line futures, Dated Brent versus Dubai first-line futures and Brent first-line versus Dubai first-line futures. One lot is equivalent to 1,000 barrels.
The lower traded volume came as Dated Brent versus Dubai first-line futures volume fell 7.72% month over month to 14,424 lots, and Brent first-line versus Dubai first-line futures volume dropped 2.17% to 817,557 lots, even as Dubai first-line futures volume rose 1.08% to 1.595 million lots, exchange data showed.
Traders attributed the decline to reduced Murban-Dubai positioning following the suspension of the IFAD Murban crude futures contract.
Middle East crude supply was volatile in September, with the shutdown of Saudi Arabia's East-West pipeline and reduced Iranian crude exports sending regional cash differentials to multimonth highs. Prices, however, cooled toward the end of the month as producers and traders ramped up spot offers.
Meanwhile, combined open interest in Dubai crude futures totaled 901,156 lots on Sept. 30, down from 1.054 million lots at the end of August, according to exchange data.
The lower combined open interest across Dubai crude futures

came as Dubai first-line futures open interest dropped 14.31% month over month to 502,873 lots, Dated Brent versus Dubai first-line futures open interest fell 17.50% to 25,347 lots and Brent first-line versus Dubai first-line futures open interest dropped 14.54% to 372,936 lots, exchange data showed.
Open interest in the front-month September Dubai crude futures contract on ICE totaled 224,809 lots at the end of September, down 4% month over month, according to exchange data.
Spot trading activity in October is expected to be thin due to China's Golden Week holiday over Oct. 1-7, although market participants will be closely monitoring vessel transits through the Strait of Hormuz amid an uptick in voyages through the channel in September.
November cash Dubai assessments averaged $113.711/barrel in September, up from an average of $88.75/b for front-month October cash Dubai in August, while the Dubai cash-futures spread -- a measure of the market structure -- averaged $21.60/b in September, up from $9.59/b in August, data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, showed.
-- Yong ren Toh, Yue Wang

CRUDE MOC: Middle East crude differentials hit 5-week low
Platts Dubai premium down $2.88/b
Platts Oman premium down $3.28/b
Four Dubai partials trade during MOC

Cash differentials for Middle East sour crude markers slid for a second day during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 6, hitting lows not seen since late August.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai and cash Oman both at a premium of $10.58/b to samemonth Dubai futures at the market close, down $2.88/b and $3.28/b respectively on the day.
December cash Murban was down $2.68/b over the same period at a premium of $10.78/b to same-month Dubai futures.
During the MOC, four December Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded, with Mercuria selling all four partials to Trafigura.

Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
WTI Midland climbs further as transatlantic freight hits record highs
Volatile freight economics add to uncertainty around mediumterm supply prospects

North Sea crude values strengthened further Oct. 6 as transatlantic freight rates reached all-time highs, with volatile

freight economics casting further uncertainty on bearish predictions ahead.
Landed values for WTI Midland continued to climb, as unprecedented tightening in tonnage sent Aframax freight rates on the US Gulf Coast-UK Continent route to their highest on record since Platts launched the assessment in 2018.
The Platts Market on Close assessment process saw bids shown for all six Dated Brent basket grades on both a FOB and CIF basis -- notably, bids for WTI Midland CIF Rotterdam for end-October to early-November arrival were left outstanding without finding a seller at premiums as high as $22.65/b over Dated Brent.
"There isn't a lot of WTI Midland around in the first half of November, let alone the third decade of October," said a Europebased North Sea crude trader, adding, however, that in line with the trading cycle for freshly resupplied barrels, there was still availability for arrival in the second half of November.
In line with the volatility surrounding freight economics, traders described values for late-November arrivals of WTI Midland as fluctuating heavily during European afternoon trading, depending on when deals were finalized.
A similar uncertainty was seen across the sour segment, with liquidity across Norway's Johan Sverdrup grinding to a halt as market participants struggled to value barrels landing in the November cycle.
"JS is not trading, nobody knows how to value anything with freight into the region so high, but FOB differentials are also in freefall," said a second trader.
As such, medium-term prospects in the region remained up in the air. "The curves still imply that differentials come off sharply after this week," said a third trader, referring to the steeply backwardated structure between the Brent CFDs settling across prompt weeks.
However, "we're not sure how sharp [the correction] will be with current market conditions, the uncertainty is just too big," the trader added.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Natasha Tan
Americas Crude
Brazil's PPSA to sell 4 million barrels of crude oil at Oct. 7 spot market auction
Output from Buzios field
Continues ninth spot sale
Weak interest in previous auctions
Brazilian government subsalt management company PPSA plans to sell 4 million barrels of crude from the Buzios production sharing field at a spot-market auction Oct. 7, according to the regulator.
The spot-market auction will be a continuation of PPSA's ninth sale held Sept. 30, with the cargoes up for bid failing to sell during the previous sale, PPSA said in the auction notice published Oct.

5

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 6, 2026

5. The available crude from Buzios will be grouped in five lots, with offloads expected to take place in November and December.
China National Offshore Oil Corp., Equinor, ExxonMobil, Galp Energia, Petrobras, PetroChina, Prio, Shell and TotalEnergies were invited to participate in the auction, PPSA said. The companies were also invited to participate in the first portion of the ninth spot market auction held on Sept. 30.
The first lot will cover a single 500,000-barrel cargo from the FPSO P-74 floating production, storage and offloading vessel installed at Buzios, PPSA said. The offload is scheduled to take place Nov. 12-13.
The second lot will feature two cargoes of 500,000 barrels, with a single cargo each from the FPSOs P-76 and P-77, PPSA said. Offloads were scheduled to take place Nov. 11-12. The auction's third lot will feature a single cargo of 500,000 barrels from the FPSO P-75 installed at Buzios, with the offload expected to take place in December. PPSA said.
The auction's fourth lot will feature two cargos of 500,000 barrels, with a single cargo each from the FPSOs P-76 and P-77 to be offloaded in December, PPSA said. The fifth and final lot will cover a single cargo of 1 million barrels to be offloaded from the FPSO Almirante Barroso in December.
The winning bids will be benchmarked to Dated Brent prices, PPSA said.
Market volatility PPSA's recent auctions have faced weak demand amid global
oil price volatility and high shipping rates driven by the war in Iran. During the Sept. 30 spot market auction, PPSA sold just 2
million barrels of the 6 million barrels put up for sale, according to the regulator. In August, meanwhile, PPSA sold just 4.9 million barrels of the 12.9 million barrels on offer.
In addition, PPSA has tabled plans to hold an auction to sell about 106.5 million barrels representing next year's share of production sharing output from several subsalt fields under long-term contracts, according to the regulator. PPSA had initially scheduled the sale for July 29 before pushing the auction back until Aug. 26. So far, PPSA hasn't set another date for the auction.
The uncertainties could force PPSA to increasingly rely on spot market auctions going forward, according to industry and government officials. PPSA has repeatedly turned to the spot market in 2026 to sell the government's share of production amid skyrocketing production growth in the subsalt region.
PPSA has registered a series of production records in recent months after the installation and ramp up of six new FPSOs installed since October 2024, according to the regulator. That included first oil from the Bacalhau field in the Norte de Carcara production sharing area in October 2025, as well as first oil from the FPSOs P-78 and P-79 installed at Buzios on Dec. 31 and May 1, respectively.
Brazil's production sharing fields produced a record 1.843 million b/d in July, up 2.8% from the previous record of 1.792 million b/d set in June, PPSA said in its latest production report released Sept. 23. Brazil received 301,260 b/d from production

sharing fields in July, down slightly from a record 306,100 b/d in June.
Buzios was the country's top oil producer in August, remaining above 1.0 million b/d for the third consecutive month, Brazil's National Petroleum Agency said in a production report released Oct. 1. Buzios produced 1.172 million b/d in August.
PPSA has not yet released its report on August production sharing output.
-- Jeff Fick

Platts US Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
Gulf Coast unleaded differentials weaken
Chicago buying interest drives prices higher
LA CARBOB rises 5 cents amid refinery flaring
In the US gasoline market, multiple West Coast refineries reported flaring as Chicago and Group 3 differentials diverged on Oct. 6. In the Gulf Coast region, alkylate barge values closed the day sharply weaker while New York Harbor barges tracked NYMEX RBOB futures higher.
Gulf Coast US Gulf Coast gasoline differentials weakened Oct. 6, with
unleaded grades posting the sharpest losses as the market rolled Colonial Pipeline cycles and alkylate traded lower.
Platts assessed the benchmark unleaded 87 M-grade regular gasoline at NYMEX November RBOB futures plus 25 cents/gal, 2.75 cents weaker after the grade was offered lower day over day in the Platts Market on Close assessment process.
Unleaded 93 V-grade dipped further, falling to futures plus 79.75 cents/gal, 9.75 cents weaker from the previous trading day. The move in the higher-octane unleaded grade comes alongside weaker alkylate DAP USGC barge values. Platts assessed the blend stock at NYMEX November RBOB futures plus 65.50 cents/ gal Oct. 6, sharply lower from the 90-cent/gal premium assessed the previous day.
The move coincides with stronger gasoline crack spreads. Houston cracks improved throughout September as the Gulf Coast region continues to send large amounts of gasoline volumes to the East and West Coasts, according to S&P Global CERA analysis.
As of Oct. 6, the unleaded 87 M-grade regular gasoline crack spread averaged $59.18/barrel versus West Texas Intermediate crude in Houston, the latest Platts Refining Margin report showed.
Atlantic Coast On the Atlantic Coast, differentials for New York Harbor barges
fell on the day as outright prices rose. Platts assessed 13.5 RVP RBOB NYH barges at NYMEX
November RBOB futures plus 13.45 cents/gallon, down 0.55 cent

Platts US Jet Fuel Daily Market Analysis
USGC jet discount widens by 3.00 cents/gal
USAC jet premium drops 0.75 cent/gal

US Gulf and Atlantic Coast jet differentials were assessed lower on Oct. 6, after NYMEX ULSD futures rallied amid a backwardated market structure.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed US Gulf Coast jet for Colonial Pipeline's 59th cycle at NYMEX November ULSD futures minus 11.50 cents/gal on Oct. 6, down by 3.00 cents/gal on the day, based on trades at the same level seen in the Platts Market on Close assessment process.
The grade in the amount of 100,000 barrels was seen traded at discounts to November ULSD futures between 11.50 cents/gal and 11.25 cents/gal in the MOC.
Prompt barrels were heard valued at premiums to forward cycle 60 between 2.00 cents/gal and 5.00 cents/gal in the MOC.
US Atlantic Coast jet for Buckeye Pipeline was assessed at NYMEX November ULSD futures plus 16.75 cents/gal on Oct. 6, down 0.75 cent/gal on the day, based on an offer at plus 17.00 cents/gal and a backwardated market structure.
The USAC to USGC jet premium rose to 28.25 cents/gal on Oct. 6, historically high enough to support the arbitrage on Colonial Pipeline to New York Harbor.
US West Coast jet for Los Angeles pipeline fell by 5.25 cents/gal on the day to NYMEX November ULSD futures minus 2.25 cents/ gal on Oct. 6, based on a bid at minus 2.50 cents/gal in the MOC.
Asia Pacific refined products prices could draw support from a Chines export ban. Chinese refiners have halted clean oil product exports to foreign countries, two refining sources and two traders with knowledge of the matter said Oct. 1-2, adding that the suspension could extend into November amid lower refinery runs.
However, the biggest exporter to the USWC for jet fuel is South Korea.
South Korea's refining industry expects stable crude procurement through year-end, supported by government measures, enabling major refiners to sustain clean oil product exports to Asia-Pacific customers and help cover essential fuel shortfalls across the region, according to refinery sources, Korea Petroleum Association, and the trade ministry over Sept.29-Oct 5.

Diesel
US diesel export ban could lift prices with 'buffers' growing thin: Chevron CEO
Export curbs would send 'bad signal' to allies
Oil market fundamentals 'remain distressed'
Iraq, Venezuela offer growth opportunities
A US diesel export ban would be unlikely to reduce domestic prices and would send a "very bad signal" to America's allies, Chevron CEO Mike Wirth said Oct. 6, adding that the buffers that have cushioned the market impact of the US-Iran war are "much thinner today."
Speaking at the Energy Intelligence Forum in London following weeks of speculation about a potential ban on US diesel exports,

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

7

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 6, 2026

Wirth said such a move could in fact increase diesel prices in some parts of the US.
According to reports, the Trump administration has been considering a ban amid surging diesel prices ahead of the pivotal midterm elections in November, although speculation over a ban has been tempered somewhat in recent days by a planned refined product stock release by members of the International Energy Agency.
"The issue that we are facing right now in the energy system are constraints on supply," Wirth said. "The system responds with workarounds," from the US easing marine transportation bans to Persian Gulf exporters finding new ways to get oil to markets.
"Actions that increase flexibility in the system, that increase supply are the actions that allow the market then to work. Restricting the supply, which an export ban would do, is the reverse," he added. "The US is not independent of world markets, it is interconnected. It exports diesel off the Gulf Coast, it imports diesel into the east and west coasts."
"What might on the very surface feel like a thing that would help US consumers, particularly when there are concerns over upcoming elections and the politics involved, might not have the desired effect," Wirth said. "You could see some upward pressure on prices in some parts of the country."
"And I think particularly for our allies it would send a very bad signal, when the US has been a reliable supplier in a time of need... we need to continue to serve that role."
Oil demand growth Addressing wider market dynamics, Wirth said Chevron has
not "fundamentally changed" its view on oil demand, despite the war in the Middle East.
He noted that "buffers", including a 5 million barrel/day reduction in Chinese oil buying, high pre-war inventories, strong supply at the start of the year and a large quantity of sanctioned oil on water that previously struggled to find a home, had helped temper price spikes.
"The reality is those buffers are much thinner today than they were when this began," the Chevron boss said. "Releases on the products side will help mitigate some of the near-term pressure, but underlying fundamentals remain distressed and the system is more fragile today than earlier on in the situation."
While it is too early to say when those buffers might run out, Wirth said, "the pressure continues to be to the upside", with the "underlying fundamentals... becoming tighter and tighter."
Indeed, the physical price of oil in Asia today is "much closer to $150/b than to $100/b," he said, noting that futures prices are "a very poor indicator" of supply-demand fundamentals.
In the long term, Wirth said energy affordability and reliability were back on the agenda for governments across the world. Energy demand will continue to be lifted, he said, by population growth, economic development, and AI's voracious electricity demand.

International opportunities While the Iran war is providing a windfall for oil and gas
producers like Chevron, Wirth said a price correction will come eventually.
As a result, additional revenue will be used for organic growth, as well as to strengthen the balance sheet to support Chevron through the cycles, he said.
Internationally, the company is still in negotiations with Iraq's government over West Qurna and Nasiriyah, both "prolific hydrocarbon regions" that were previously rendered uncompetitive for Chevron by Iraq's fiscal terms.
"The economics look better today," Wirth said. "Iraq is attractive, we have had very good engagement with the government there, and I am very optimistic that those negotiations will lead to a good deal." A result is still months away, he noted.
The company is also open to participating in new midstream infrastructure, with Baghdad eager to build a pipeline to Syria to supply crude to the Mediterranean market, which Wirth said could make Iraqi oil projects even more attractive.
While Chevron would not build such a system on its own, "we could be one part of a multi-party consortium that could do something like that," he said.
In addition, Wirth was bullish on the prospects for Venezuela, where Chevron was operating even before Nicolas Maduro was removed from power in January.
"We made the decision to stay and it put us in a position where now with things moving in a different direction we are advantaged," Wirth said, noting that Chevron produces 300,000 b/d there through its joint ventures.
With significantly improved fiscal terms under the new government of Delcy Rodriguez, Chevron intends to double production in the next five years, he said.
But he said "it will take time and money, and a lot of both, for [Venezuela] to get back up to 3 million b/d", from some 1 million b/d today. "You'll need to see more companies like ours come in," he said.
On the exploration side, Wirth said the company had done more infrastructure-led, and less frontier exploration, in the previous cycle, but pointed to increased industry interest in frontiers, driven by exploration successes in Guyana and Namibia.
"I don't think the last big basin has been found yet," Wirth said. "I think there is another one out there."
-- Charlie Mitchell
European diesel prices plunge on hopes of resupply from G7 stock release
10 ppm diesel CIF NWE falls $70.50/mt in a week
Prices could come off quite significantly: trader
European diesel prices have fallen sharply after the G7 announced oil stock release, fuelling expectations of fresh supply into a market that has been running tight.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

8

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 6, 2026

Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the 10 ppm diesel CIF NWE cargo at $1,397.75/metric ton on Oct. 5, down $70.50/ mt week over week and well below the September average of $1,503.75/mt.
The downturn was largely driven by a bearish trend in the underlying gasoil futures contract.
Platts assessed the Mo1 ICE low sulfur gasoil futures down $95.75/mt on Oct. 2, following the G7's announcement that it had given its member states a four- month deadline to release 100 million barrels of oil stocks. This marked the largest sell- off in ICE LSGO futures since Aug. 3 and the lowest price since Aug. 28, according to Platts data.
Platts assessed the contract up $28/mt at $1,383.50/mt on Oct. 5, but it remains well below the September average of $1,465.25/mt.
"Physical players are cutting long positions, ahead of extra flows," said a European diesel market source.
Some market participants are also anticipating further price declines when the product reaches the market.
"It's quite a significant volume that would be available," said a European diesel trader.
The trader added that "prices could come off quite significantly when the stocks are released," but cautioned that "other releases have been slow to reach the market...and the question is whether the tenders go ahead."
Meanwhile, other market participants have been more skeptical of the long- term price impact of the oil stock release.
"It does look like a temporary fix rather than a real solution," said a Mediterranean diesel trader.
Additionally, the falling flat price could "stimulate some demand," according to the first trader. Stronger demand could, in turn, tighten fundamentals despite greater supply.
-- Sophia Aung
Russia mulling partial easing of diesel export ban: report
Some producers might be allowed to export this month
Winter fuel switch prompts storage concerns for summer grade
Russia could partially relax its diesel export ban by allowing some producers to export the product, Interfax news agency reported Oct. 6, citing sources.
This measure could be applied in October in order to prevent refineries from overstocking, the report said.
Last week, the Russian government extended its full ban on the export of diesel until the end of October.
Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak said at the time that Russia could consider partially opening up diesel exports once the country returned to a production surplus.
In 2023, the authorities lifted a diesel ban only a few weeks after enforcing it due to a lack of storage capacity.
Russia is in the process of switching to winter-grade diesel,

with demand for summer grades winding down, according to market sources. Any summer diesel still being produced will take up storage space, so lifting the ban would be preferable.
Demand for summer product has gradually diminished as autumn harvest work draws to a close, according to market participants. Also, diesel supply at retail stations and on the spot market is sufficient to cover demand, according to sources
Russia enforced a full diesel export ban on July 9, by including big refineries in an already existing prohibition on so-called nonproducers, which include traders and small refineries that process less than 1 million metric tons/year. The ban was initially valid until the end of August but was extended to the end of September amid shortages caused by ongoing Ukrainian strikes on refineries. The ban on non-producers is in force until the end of January 2027.
-- Elza Turner
Marine Fuel
Gibraltar, Algeciras bunker Hi-5 spreads hit 6-month lows on HSFO supply constraints
Supplier unable to supply HSFO bunkers in Algeciras due to lack of product
HSFO availabilities in Gibraltar tight across suppliers: source
Bulk VLSFO market well-supplied amid closed arbitrage windows
The Gibraltar and Algeciras delivered bunker Hi-5 spreads - the difference between very low sulfur and high sulfur fuel oil - fell to six-month lows on Oct. 6 as rising HSFO prices outpaced gains in VLSFO due to supply tightness.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Hi-5 spread in Gibraltar at $31/metric ton on Oct. 6, as HSFO increased 4.12%, while VLSFO increased 0.14%. The Hi-5 spread in nearby Algeciras was assessed at $22/mt on Oct. 6.
The narrowing was driven by tightening HSFO fundamentals at both ports, with market participants reporting constrained availability due to supply-side issues rather than any pickup in demand.
A supplier in Algeciras said the "market is a bit short," adding they were "supplying our contracts only ... not offering on the spot market."
The withdrawal of at least one supplier from spot market activity suggested that tightness could persist in the near term, with implications for vessel operators seeking HSFO stems on short notice in the region.
A Gibraltar-based supplier said there was limited availability of HSFO at the port among participants, confirming the tightness was supply-driven.
Demand at both ports was described as subdued, which market participants said had offset more pronounced price movements.
In the bulk Mediterranean high sulfur fuel oil market, perspectives on supply were divided.

One trader described the market as "dry" with "good demand, not many barrels," and expected those tight conditions to persist throughout the month of October.
However, a second Mediterranean-based trader saw a balanced market instead, noting thin trading activity but no supply concerns, supported by steady arrivals from Latin America.
Within the bulk VLSFO markets, closed arbitrage windows out of Europe and a heavier feedstock market have increased the availability of 0.5% material, putting pressure on Hi-5 spreads across the region.
Market participants said that delivered VLSFO cargo premiums into the West Mediterranean softened into the low$30/mt range from the high-$30s previously in the summer, reflecting adequate supply.

Resid
ICE front-month Singapore HSFO open interest falls 1.8% MOM in Sep
380 CST contract drives total open interest decline
HSFO total open interest falls to four-year low
Asian HSFO market strengthens on tighter supply
The combined open interest for front-month Singapore high sulfur fuel oil contracts fell 1.8% month over month in September to 8.479 million metric tons as of Sept. 30, Intercontinental Exchange data showed Oct. 6.
The combined open interest includes contracts for the Singapore 380 CST and 180 CST HSFO grades, the Singapore viscosity spread and the Singapore 180 CST and 380 CST EastWest contracts.
Open interest for the front-month Singapore 380 CST contract fell 3.8% to 5.337 million mt, contributing to the decline in total front-month open interest.
Open interest across the HSFO forward curve at the end of September reached a nearly four-year low of 33.447 million mt, down from 36.704 million mt on Aug. 31. Total open interest across the HSFO forward curve was last lower at 32.990 million mt on Jan. 31, 2023.
The Asian HSFO market continues to draw strength from tighter supply and robust bunker demand, while LNG shortages in the wake of the Middle East conflict could continue to underpin utility-sector HSFO demand even as the summer power generation season ends.
In addition to supply disruptions in the Persian Gulf, the latest Enterprise Singapore data showed that Singapore recorded no fuel oil imports from Russia for the 13th consecutive week.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Singapore 380 CST HSFO cargo's cash differential to the MOPS 380 CST HSFO assessment at a premium of $45.42/mt at the Asian close Oct. 5, up 17 cents/mt from Oct. 2. The benchmark HSFO cash

differential was about 68% higher compared with a month earlier, Platts data showed.
The Singapore 380 CST HSFO cargo price, assessed at $714.53/ mt at the Asian close on Oct. 5, averaged $650.77/mt in September, compared with $575.30/mt in August, according to Platts data.

Marine fuel oil The combined open interest for front-month Singapore marine
fuel oil contracts on ICE fell 1.3% to 3.931 million mt on Sept. 30. The combined open interest includes contracts for Singapore
marine fuel oil 0.5%S, the marine fuel 0.5%S-380 CST HSFO spread -- called the Hi-5 spread -- and the marine fuel 0.5%S East-West contact.
The decline was led by a 2.1% decrease in open interest for the marine fuel 0.5%S contract, which fell to 2.699 million mt.
In contrast, open interest for the marine fuel oil contracts across the forward curve rose 8.2% month over month to 23.737 million mt, recovering from a more than three-year low in August.
The Asian low sulfur fuel oil market could find some support from limited prompt availability of finished-grade supplies, although arbitrage arrivals of blending components from Western markets in the second half of October and the first half of November could limit the drawdown in regional stockpiles.

Feedstocks
Platts European Naphtha Daily Market Analysis
CIF NWE naphtha cargo prices drop $16.25/mt
October/November spread widens to $32.50/mt
The European naphtha complex retreated Oct. 6, with CIF Northwest European cargo values falling on the day while the front-month crack continued to strengthen.
Prompt structure also firmed, with the October/November spread widening on the day, pointing to continued tightness in nearby availability despite softer downstream demand signals.
Petrochemical demand offered little support. Europe's October ethylene contract settled 80/mt higher at 1,555/mt, but downstream polyethylene gains were largely feedstock-cost driven, with buyers still limiting purchases to immediate needs.
Kaub levels remained critically low, falling to 1 cm Oct. 6 at 13:00 CET, with improvement not expected to resolve barge logistics in the week ahead, according to German water authority WSV data.
Gasoline blending interest has weakened post-summer, while elevated flat prices have reduced buying appetite. Tight highoctane component supply has also limited blenders' ability to absorb larger volumes of lower-octane naphtha.
Export economics also remained difficult, with elevated clean tanker freight limiting long-haul gasoline flows and reducing related naphtha blending demand.
Outside Europe, CFR Japan naphtha swap open interest fell to a 22-month low in September, pointing to more cautious positioning in the wider naphtha complex, while South Korean cracker rationalization added to pressure on naphtha-fed petrochemical demand.
Overall, weaker flat prices contrasted with a firmer crack and steeper prompt backwardation, as tight supply and logistics constraints continued to support the front of the European naphtha market, while subdued blending and petrochemical demand capped broader upside.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the physical naphtha CIF NWE cargo price at $855.75/mt, down $16.25/mt on the day.
Platts assessed the front-month CIF NWE naphtha crack at minus $4.30/b, up 95 cents/b day over day, with the equivalent swap down $17.50/mt at $814.25/mt.
Platts assessed the October/November time spread at $32.50/mt, up $2.50/mt on the day, while the November/ December spread was steady day over day at $27.50/mt.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Harry Deighton

Gas Liquids
China's LPG imports fall 15% MOM in September; US remains top supplier: CAS
US supplies 51% of China's September LPG imports
Iranian shipments fall nearly 60% to 161,000 mt
Canadian deliveries rise 61% month over month
China's LPG imports declined 15.07% month over month to 2.339 million metric tons in September, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data as of Oct. 6.
September's imports were down 26.42% year over year, marking the fifth consecutive month of weaker annual comparisons, the CAS data showed.
The US remained China's largest LPG supplier in September, shipping 1.200 million mt, down 13.42% from 1.386 million mt in August. US cargoes accounted for 51.3% of China's total LPG imports in September, up from 50.3% a month earlier, as the decline in overall inflows lifted the US market share despite lower absolute volumes.
Canada was China's second-largest LPG supplier in September, delivering 281,000 mt, up 60.57% from 175,000 mt in August. Its share of China's total LPG imports rose to 12.0% from 6.4% the previous month.
Iran's LPG shipments to China fell 59.75% month over month to 161,000 mt in September. Iran accounted for 6.9% of China's total LPG imports, down from 14.5% in August, according to the CAS data.
Middle East-origin LPG volumes totaled 344,000 mt in September, down 50.1% from 689,000 mt in August, the CAS data showed.
Propane accounted for the bulk of China's September LPG imports at 2.006 million mt, down 9.84% from 2.225 million mt in August. Butane imports fell 37.05% month over month to 333,000 mt.
Propane represented 85.8% of total China LPG imports in September, up from 80.8% in August, while butane's share declined to 14.2% from 19.2%, the CAS data showed.
Beijing excluded US crude and propane from the US-China agreement on a $30-billion tariff-reduction framework reached during the Sept. 23-25 summit between US President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping, according to a statement released by China's Ministry of Commerce Sept. 28, as reported earlier by Platts.
The tariffs on US crude and LPG are unlikely to be adjusted in the short term as the US and China have extended their bilateral trade truce through Jan. 10, 2027, the report showed.
Earlier, China's LPG imports fell 15.6% month over month to 2.79 million mt in August from a 12-month high in July, though volumes remained the highest for any month in 2026 so far, according to General Administration of Customs data and trade estimates, Platts reported Sept. 23.
The decline is expected to tighten feedstock availability for China's propane dehydrogenation sector, which relies heavily

Asian, Middle East propane swap trades on CME rises 31% MOM in September
Propane FEI futures traded volume up 85.53% MOM
Propane FEI futures open interest slips MOM in Sept
The total volume of Asian and Middle Eastern traded propane swaps settled on the Chicago Mercantile Exchange rose 31.11% month over month to 3.199 million metric tons in September, according to the latest data on CME's website seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy on Oct. 5.
The volume for propane Saudi CP futures traded on the CME increased 83.53% month over month to 468,000 mt, while trading volumes for propane FEI futures traded on the CME rose 24.99% over the same period to 2.731 million mt, the data showed. Year over year, Saudi CP futures saw a 73.30% decline in traded volumes, while propane FEI futures similarly fell 67.30% over the same period.
Propane prices rose over the same period, with Platts assessing the front-month CFR North Asia propane swap at an average of $831.55/mt in September, up 17.83% from the August average of $705.73/mt.
Platts assessed the front-month FOB Saudi CP swaps at an average of $650.91/mt in September, up 9.62% from $593.80/mt in August.
Given the ongoing war against Iran in the Middle East and the subsequent closure of the Strait of Hormuz as well as disruption to cargo flows along the Bab Al-Mandeb, propane supplies out of the second-largest producing region after the US have declined significantly.
Traditional customers of Middle Eastern LPG have had little choice but to shift to importing from alternative sources, such as the US.
According to a report by S&P Global Commodities at Sea analysts, the growing export trend for US propane is expected to

continue into October. "October exports are projected to be higher in September,
thanks to the favorable arbitrage economics in September," the report showed.
Meanwhile, the total open interest for front-month October Asia and Middle East propane contracts on the CME stood at 712,000 mt in September, CME data showed.
Open interest for front-month October FEI propane futures slipped 0.18% month over month to 559,000 in September, while open interest for front-month October Saudi CP propane futures fell 6.13% to 153,000 mt over the same period.

Platts East of Suez Dirty Tanker Daily Commentary
VLCC firm on tight supply, strong demand
Suezmax gains as charterers seek alternatives
Aframax higher on bullish sentiment, Western strength
The East of Suez VLCC market remained firm Oct. 6, with sentiment showing little sign of easing.
Market participants said the next fixture could be concluded at a higher level, a scenario many viewed as entirely plausible.
The firmer West market, including Brazil, where recent fixtures were concluded above the previous level, was seen by sources as providing firm support for the broader VLCC market.
Fixture-wise, ST was heard to have placed Koch's VLCC Silverstone on subjects for an Oct. 19 Fujairah-Singapore voyage at w875, with a Far East discharge option at w825.
Fresh cargoes included an IOC inquiry seeking a VLCC for an Oct. 21 loading from Basrah to Sohar STS, with discharge options via Vadinar STS or Paradip.
S-Oil was also heard to be seeking a VLCC for an Oct. 22 loading from a Gulf of Oman STS to the West Coast India STS range to Onsan.

Platts Americas Clean Tanker Daily Commentary
Tanker rates drop amid low shipping demand
Sources expect continued decline through week
Sentiment remained bearish for the Americas Medium Range clean tanker rates Oct. 6, after market activity remained limited, with no outstanding cargoes reported as seeking to load in the US Gulf Coast during the day.
This led market sources to maintain expectations that rates would continue to decline from last-done levels, with some indicating that bearish sentiment could persist throughout the week.
"I don't know how long sentiment will hold. If I had a prompt ship, I would be looking to fix at next done and get the thing moving," a shipbroker said. "Holiday weekend this weekend, so we may see a flurry of activity, with dropping rates to the end of the week."
The bearish sentiment was reflected in a recent deal in which Trafigura placed the PS Imabari on subjects for an Oct. 9-10 USGC loading run, with a transatlantic option at w365 and East Coast South America at w450.
However, the bulk of market indications heard after the deal fell below the achieved levels, with most calls at w360 for the transatlantic runs and w435 for the East Coast South America voyages.
Platts assessed rates for the benchmark 38,000 mt USGC-UK Continent run at w360 and freight for the USGC-Brazil route at w435, down w15 for both routes on the day.
This decline also drove the decreases seen on the lump sum routes, despite a lack of freight tests on the short- and long-haul routes during the first days of the week.
Platts assessed rates for the USGC-Caribbean run at $1.25 million and freight for the USGC-Chile route at $3.8 million, down $100,000 for both routes on the day.

News
US EIA sharply raises oil price forecast on Middle East supply disruptions
Agency lifts Q4 Brent forecast $14/b to $105/b
Tight supply pushes US diesel above $6/gal, pressures crude
Middle East disruptions cut output 4.5 million b/d
The US Energy Information Administration has sharply raised its near-term oil price forecast, projecting crude will average $105/barrel in the fourth quarter of 2026 as Middle East supply disruptions drain global inventories and tight diesel markets amplify upward price pressure.
The EIA's October Short-Term Energy Outlook marked a $14/b upward revision from last month's forecast and followed a surge in Dated Brent to an average of $114/b in September -- a $23/b jump from August's average. The agency forecasts global oil inventories will fall by a further 700,000 barrels/day in Q4 after declining by 1.9 million b/d in Q3, a trajectory that points to sustained tightness in physical crude and products markets through the end of the year.
The agency said oil and product flows from the Middle East were likely to "generally increase" due to "a combination of convoys through the Strait of Hormuz and workarounds to oil exports ... including bypass routes and increased use of shipto-ship transfers." However, the threat of more regional attacks and other market disruptions was likely to hold prices higher for longer than previously forecast.
"Although we expect oil production and flows from the Middle East will increase, we raised our global oil price forecast compared with last month," EIA said. "The attacks on the EastWest pipeline in Saudi Arabia highlight the potential for continued volatility in physical oil flows and oil amid ongoing withdrawals of oil inventories globally."
Meanwhile, "extreme tightness" in diesel markets would continue to raise demand for crude among refiners hoping to meet diesel demand, EIA wrote. The agency forecast Dated Brent to fall to an average of $84/b in 2027.
Diesel pressure Distillate markets are at the center of the current price
rally, EIA said. US retail diesel prices averaged $6.29/gallon in September, and the EIA expects prices to remain above $6/ gal through October before gradually easing to an average of around $4.50/gal in 2027. East Coast distillate inventories stood 32% below their five-year seasonal average in September and are forecast to remain between 20% and 30% below average throughout the winter -- a persistent supply deficit that will keep heating oil and diesel markets exposed to further shocks.
Record diesel prices have become a fixture of US political messaging in recent weeks. On Oct. 5, US President Donald Trump signed an executive order temporarily lifting restrictions that prohibit the use of dyed diesel, which is typically limited to

off-road use and not subject to a federal excise tax, in his bid to help farmers and transporters grappling with fuel costs.
The administration has faced weeks of pressure from Republican legislators and candidates in the Nov. 2 midterm elections to restrict US diesel exports. Trump expressed support for the idea on Sept. 22, one day before US Energy Secretary Chris Wright denied reports that the administration was preparing a 90-day ban on all diesel exports. Instead, Wright said, the administration was hoping to urge refiners to voluntarily reroute some flows and "trying to avoid a blunt hammer" in its attempts to relieve upward price pressure.
A total ban could force American refiners to slash crude runs by nearly 2 million b/d and ultimately drive up domestic gasoline prices, an analysis from S&P Global CERA forecast Sept. 22.
On the supply side, the EIA said that the gradual increase in the use of Middle East shipping workarounds would reduce regional production shut-ins from 4.5 million b/d in Q4 to 2.7 million b/d in Q1 2027.
US crude oil production is forecast to rise from 13.9 million b/d in 2026 to 14.3 million b/d in 2027, EIA said, providing an additional buffer to global supply as Middle East volumes recover.
Winter fuels The EIA's 2026-27 Winter Fuels Outlook, published alongside
the October STEO, presented what the agency called a "mixed" picture for US households, with the burden of higher energy costs falling unevenly across heating fuel types.
"Relatively high natural gas inventories entering the winter season provide a cushion for heating demand, while households that rely on heating oil face higher prices because distillate markets remain tight," EIA Administrator Tristan Abbey said in a statement.
Households that primarily heat with natural gas or propane -- about half of all US households, according to the EIA -- are expected to spend less this winter than last, supported by natural gas inventories that are forecast to enter the heating season 2% above the previous five-year average. Strong production growth is expected to limit price increases even as LNG exports rise, with the Henry Hub spot price forecast to average $3.16 per million Btu in 2027, down 9% from 2026.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

14

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 6, 2026

The outlook would be considerably more challenging for the roughly 3% of US households that rely on heating oil, concentrated largely in the Northeast, EIA forecast. Those consumers would pay about 21% more for heating oil this winter than last, the agency expected, driven by a projected 30% increase in heating oil prices, only partially offset by a forecast of milder weather in the region.
Electricity users are also expected to face higher bills. Wholesale electricity prices are forecast to average $52 per megawatt-hour in 2026, 11% higher than in 2025, with PJM wholesale prices rising 41% -- though Northwest Mid-Columbia prices are forecast to fall 23%. Record-breaking summer temperatures drove a 4% increase in electricity consumption in Q3 2026 compared with the same period last year.
US retail gasoline prices averaged $4.35/gal in September. The EIA forecasts retail gasoline prices will average $3.91/gal in 2026 before easing to $3.56/gal in 2027, following expected declines in crude.
-- Eamonn Brennan
Indonesia fuel import tenders draw few bidders amid supply disruption: ministry
Middle East conflict disrupts supply chains
Jakarta seeks alternative supplies at higher costs
Import sources drop 25% since conflict began
Indonesia's fuel import tenders are attracting few or no participants as the Middle East conflict disrupts regional supply chains, forcing Jakarta to seek alternative suppliers at potentially higher costs, Laode Sulaeman, director general for oil and gas at the Energy and Mineral Resources Ministry, said at a government meeting Oct. 5.
Sulaeman said that state-owned Pertamina is increasingly sourcing cargoes outside its normal tender process.
"Previously, Pertamina held tenders, but now there are relatively few participants in the tenders. So we have to look for countries that have surplus stocks," Sulaeman said.
"We may have to buy at prices that are potentially higher than what we would pay through the usual procurement process," he added.
Indonesia has lost around 25% of its oil import sources since the conflict in the Middle East began, he said, without specifying which suppliers or volumes were affected.
Singapore was Indonesia's top gasoline supplier, accounting for more than 57% of the total gasoline and blendstocks discharged into Indonesia in 2025, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data. So far this year, Singapore has accounted for 67% of Indonesia's gasoline and blendstock discharges, while the country has added supplies from Latvia, Brunei and Qatar, none of which loaded gasoline cargoes discharged in Indonesia in 2025.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Gasoline Unl 92 FOB Singapore Cargo at $146.16/barrel on Oct. 5, down $2.15/b from the previous assessment.

The Indonesian government has repeatedly sought to diversify its supply sources since hostilities began earlier this year. Energy Minister Bahlil Lahadalia said in March that Indonesia was exploring additional crude supplies from countries including the United States, Angola, Brazil and Russia after disruptions affected flows through the Strait of Hormuz. Only about 20%25% of the country's crude imports normally transit the strait, according to the minister.
Jakarta subsequently secured a commitment for 150 million barrels of Russian crude and has discussed longer-term supply arrangements with Moscow as part of broader energy security cooperation. The government is also preparing a long-term contract for Russian crude after receiving its first shipment, Lahadalia said in August.
CAS data showed that crude and condensate loadings from the Middle East Gulf region that were discharged into Indonesia averaged 81,700 b/d in 2025, accounting for 22% of total discharges. The Middle East Gulf share has averaged 31,000 b/d so far in 2026, accounting for 10% of total discharges, while loadings from South America and the Red Sea have increased to averages of 47,000 b/d and 23,800 b/d, respectively.
Fuel stocks The government is maintaining national fuel stocks equivalent
to 18-20 days of supply, Sulaeman said, adding that any queues at fuel stations were not due to a shortage of national stocks.
One factor disrupting fuel distribution was El Niño, which had dried up some rivers used to transport fuel, requiring distributors to use trucks with less capacity than river vessels, he said.
The government is also keeping the prices of subsidized diesel and gasoline, including RON 90 Pertalite, unchanged through the end of the year to help contain inflation, he said. The fuels are widely used by farmers and fishermen, making price stability important to the government.
Unsubsidized prices to remain stable Meanwhile, Pertamina Patra Niaga Planning and Business
Growth Director Joko Pranoto said prices of unsubsidized fuels and jet fuel would remain at September levels in October. Pertamina had not fully passed higher global market costs through to domestic fuel prices in 2026, he said.
He added that domestic unsubsidized fuel and jet fuel prices remained relatively competitive with prices in neighboring countries and other markets, including Southeast Asia, Australia, Pakistan and India, despite the price shocks in March and April that followed the escalation of the conflict.
Pertamax RON 92 gasoline and Pertamax Green 95 RON 95 gasoline were kept unchanged from April before prices were raised in June, he said.
Indonesia relies heavily on imports to meet domestic fuel demand, with consumption at about 1.6 million b/d compared with domestic output of only 600,000-610,000 b/d, leaving an import requirement of about 1 million b/d, according to government data.
-- Mia Pei, Anita Nugraha

October 6, 2026

Hormuz ship crossings dip to 22 as two incidents reported in strait: CAS
Down from 26 crossings Oct. 4
Tanker struck by projectile, catches fire
Yemen forces seek to reclaim Bab al-Mandab

Ship traffic through the Strait of Hormuz dipped to 22 crossings Oct. 5, from 26 on Oct. 4, as two incidents occurred around the chokepoint that day, S&P Global Commodities at Sea said in a report Oct. 6.
Seven energy tankers made inbound transits Oct. 5, down from 15 the day prior, CAS said, while the rest of the day's crossings included six bulk carriers, one container ship and eight vessels from other segments, according to CAS.
Of the total crossings, 14 were outbound and eight inbound, CAS said, adding that 12 compliant vessels were identified to have crossed, eight of which did so without active AIS signals.
The UK Maritime Trade Organization issued two advisories Oct. 5, one of which involved a ship being struck by an unidentified projectile while transiting the strait and subsequently catching fire, according to CAS. The ship was not named by UKMTO, according to their advisory note.
According to S&P Global Maritime Intelligence Risk Suite, Greek LNG tanker Maran Gas Mystras was involved in an incident where it was struck by a projectile while transiting the Strait of Hormuz on Oct. 4. Smoke appeared to be coming from the vessel, but no injuries were reported before it proceeded to a convenience port for inspection.
Maran Gas and the Greek Ministry of Maritime Affairs & Insular Policy did not respond to an email seeking comments.
Separately, another tanker attempting to cross inbound through the strait was hailed by the Iranian Revolutionary Guard 11 nautical miles north of Khasab and instructed to turn away, which it did.
Among the successful transits of Hormuz on Oct. 5 was ADNOC's VLCC Rahman. CAS noted the ship returned to visible operations for the first time since it disappeared from AIS coverage off Fujairah over a year ago.
The Trafigura-owned Suezmax Sponde left the strait on Oct. 5 laden toward Malaysia, but its loading port and cargo remain unidentified, CAS reported. The UAE's LR1 fuel oil shuttle tanker SHT2 appeared to have exited the strait that day as well.
The Monarch Very Large Gas Carrier was the only LPG tanker to make an inbound transit on the day, entering with an inactive AIS signal and reappearing off Hamriyah, UAE, with Ruwais indicated as its next loading stop, CAS reported.
The US blockade recorded 65 vessel transits, 32 of which entered and 33 departed toward the Arabian Sea. Two outbound ships were found to be non-compliant, US-sanctioned vessels, according to CAS.
The US Central Command has redirected 130 commercial ships from its naval blockade as of Oct. 5, it said on X.

Red Sea operations In the Red Sea, Bab al-Mandab transits remained consistent
day over day at 36 crossings, comprising 15 tankers, 14 bulk carriers, four container ships and three miscellaneous vessels, CAS said.
Only 12 vessels entered the Red Sea, marking the lowest level since Aug. 3, according to CAS, while 24 vessels exited southbound.
CAS noted developments of the Yemeni government's onground operations to reclaim the Bab al-Mandab from Houthi armed militia control, as well as potential escalation in ground operations between the two parties.
The Yemeni government's armed forces have made progress in their operation, according to social media statements by the army's spokesperson, Majid al-Nuzaili, who said the army has "executed a flanking operation to gain control over the Bab alMandab Strait," Nuzaili said on X.
-- Lulwa Albuainain, Max Lin
Refinery updates
REFINERY NEWS: Diamond Green Diesel begins maintenance at Port Arthur renewable fuel plant
Refinery: DGD 3, Port Arthur, Texas
Owner: Diamond Green Diesel
Overall capacity: 470 million gal/year
Units affected: N/A
Duration: Began Oct. 5
Diamond Green Diesel (DGD) will begin maintenance activity the evening of Oct. 5, resulting in increased activity and flaring not typical of normal operations at its Port Arthur site, according to an Oct. 6 filing with the South Texas Alerting Network (STAN).
Located on the site of Valero's Port Arthur, Texas, refinery, the facility -- DGD 3 -- can produce up to 470 million gal/year of renewable fuels. DGD is a joint venture between Darling Ingredients, which supplies the feedstock, and Valero Energy, which supplied the site and the energy marketing expertise.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

16

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 6, 2026

The facility was upgraded in 2024 to make sustainable aviation fuel (SAF), which allows the facility to convert about half of its capacity -- about 235 million gal/year -- to SAF.
During its July 31 second-quarter results call, Diamond Green Diesel said it expected to produce about 335 million gallons of renewable fuel in the third quarter, about 6% below Q2 levels, but the company did not cite turnarounds or other operational issues as a reason for the drop.
A DGD spokesperson was not immediately available for comment on the length and scope of the work.
According to Valero's monthly market data, the DGD indicator on Oct. 6 averaged $3.16/gal so far for the month, up $1.97/gal, or 166%, from $1.19/gal in the second quarter of 2026. This indicates a substantial strengthening in renewable diesel economics entering the fourth quarter, supported by tight diesel supply as seasonal diesel demand for agriculture and heating rises.

The agency said oil and product flows from the Middle East were likely to "generally increase" due to "a combination of convoys through the Strait of Hormuz and workarounds to oil exports ... including bypass routes and increased use of shipto-ship transfers." However, the threat of more regional attacks and other market disruptions was likely to hold prices higher for longer than previously forecast.
-- Christopher Vanmoessner
REFINERY NEWS: New co-processing plant to start work at Cressier from 2027
Refinery: Cressier, Switzerland
Owner: VAROPreem
Overall capacity: 72,500 b/d
VAROPreem said Oct. 6 that it has made the decision to invest in a new co-processing project at Switzerland's Cressier refinery, which will enable it to use renewable and conventional feedstocks to produce biofuels.
The new plant will be operational from 2027 and will process about 40,000 tonnes of renewable feedstock a year, the company also said.
From 2020, the refinery has increased its distillation capacity and in 2023, the company commissioned a 7.7 megawatt solar farm at the site, as part of VAROPreem's investments.
Cressier refinery, which was established 60 years ago, supplies about 30% of Switzerland's fuel demand, according to the statement.
-- Elza Turner
REFINERY NEWS: Maintenance at the Wesseling part of Germany's Rhineland completed
Refinery: Rhineland (Rheinland), Shell Energy and Chemicals Park Rhineland, Germany
Owner: Shell
Overall capacity: 17 million mt/year (340,000 b/d)

EIA raises Q4 price outlook The US Energy Information Administration has sharply raised
its near-term oil price forecast, projecting crude will average $105/barrel in the fourth quarter of 2026 as Middle East supply disruptions drain global inventories and tight diesel markets amplify upward price pressure.
The EIA's October Short-Term Energy Outlook marked a $14/b upward revision from last month's forecast and followed a surge in Dated Brent to an average of $114/b in September -- a $23/b jump from August's average. The agency forecasts global oil inventories will fall by a further 700,000 barrels/day in Q4

The scheduled maintenance at the Wesseling part of Germany's Rhineland refinery has been completed "safely and on schedule", the refinery said Oct. 6.
It also said that the units have been gradually restarted and "have now returned to normal operations".
It said previously that the maintenance started in early August and would last until the end of September.
The Rhineland refinery (Shell Energy and Chemicals Park Rhineland) comprises the Wesseling (south) and Godorf (north) sites.
-- Elza Turner

REFINERY NEWS: Marathon reports unplanned flaring at Los Angeles refinery
Refinery: Los Angeles, California
Owner: Marathon Petroleum
Overall capacity: 365,000 b/d
Units affected: N/A
Duration: Oct. 4-14
Marathon Petroleum began planned work at its Los Angeles refinery on Oct. 4 that is expected to last until Oct. 14, according to a filing made with the South Coast Air Quality Management District that day.
No further information was provided about the scope of the work, which is planned for the Wilmington portion of the plant.
Earlier this month, Marathon reported unplanned flaring at its Los Angeles refinery, according to a filing to local regulators on Oct. 2.
"Due to an equipment malfunction, an unplanned emergency flaring event occurred that released 500 lb of sulfur dioxide into the atmosphere. The event is ongoing and is unrecoverable," the refiner said in a Hazardous Materials Spill Report to the Governor's Office of Emergency Services.
A company spokesperson was not immediately available for comment.
On Sept. 28, Marathon reported unplanned flaring at the plant, which caused CARBOB prices to rise.
-- Janet Mcgurty
REFINERY NEWS: ExxonMobil reports Beaumont hydrocracker upset, as USGC ULSD prices rise
Refinery: Beaumont, Texas
Owner: ExxonMobil

Overall capacity: 612,000 b/d
Units affected: hydrocracker
Duration: Oct. 4
ExxonMobil's 386,000 b/d Beaumont refinery in Texas reported an emissions event Oct. 4 after a process upset at its hydrocracker led operators to route streams to the refinery's high-pressure flare.
"Process streams were routed to the flares to minimize emissions. No off-site impacts were reported. We expect to meet all contractual commitments," said the Oct. 4 filing with local regulators.
The event began at 12:26 pm CDT and concluded at 1:30 pm, lasting about 1 hour and 4 minutes, according to a filing with the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality. The emissions were reported from the hydrocracker unit through the refinery's highpressure flare system.
The refinery has 70,000 b/d of distillate hydrocracking capacity, Energy Information Administration data showed.
The company estimated emissions of approximately 4,439 pounds of sulfur dioxide, 554 lb of carbon monoxide, 108 lb of nitrogen oxides and 48 lb of hydrogen sulfide, along with smaller quantities of hydrocarbons including butanes, pentanes, propane, propylene and unspecified VOCs.
The filing did not specify the cause of the hydrocracker upset or indicate whether refinery throughput was reduced. However, routing hydrocracker material to the flare typically reflects a unit interruption that requires operators to safely depressure equipment and stabilize operations.
US Gulf Coast diesel prices swung sharply over the past week before settling at 454.87 cents/gal on Oct. 5, a net gain of 5.37 cents/gal, as a brief sell-off triggered by news of a planned G7 oil-stock release gave way to a recovery even as the transatlantic export arbitrage weakened, according to assessments from Platts, a part of S&P Global Energy.
-- Janet Mcgurty

REFINERY NEWS: Delek US' Big Spring refinery down for 10-day maintenance
Refinery: Big Spring, Texas
Owner: Delek US
Overall capacity: 73,000 b/d
Units affected: FCCU, DHT, ULSG
Duration: Oct. 5-15
Delek US said it has taken offline for planned work a fluid catalytic cracking unit, diesel hydrotreater and ultra low sulfur gasoline unit its Big Spring, Texas, refinery for a 10-day maintenance event starting Oct. 5, triggering sulfur dioxide emissions at two flares that exceeded permitted limits, according to a filing with state regulators.
The maintenance event, which runs through Oct. 15, affected three process units -- the FCCU, the diesel hydrotreater and the ultra low sulfur gasoline unit -- at the refinery, the company said in its initial filing on Sept. 24 with the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality.
Operations will be adjusted to minimize emissions to the best extent possible, the company said. Emissions figures are based on continuous emissions monitoring system data, process knowledge, flow rates, material balance, best engineering judgment and calculations, according to the filing.
-- Janet Mcgurty
REFINERY NEWS: Valero McKee refinery restarts FCCU after work, Denver RBOB rises
Refinery: McKee refinery in Sunray, Texas
Owner: Valero
Overall capacity: 195,000 b/d
Units affected: FCCU
Duration: Oct. 6-11
Valero's McKee refinery in Sunray, Texas, began a scheduled startup of its fluid catalytic cracking unit after maintenance, triggering an opacity exceedance expected to last five days, according to a filing with state regulators.
In the filing with the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality, Valero said the start-up of the FCCU in Complex 1 will begin Oct. 6 at noon local time and is scheduled to conclude Oct. 11 at noon.

On Sept. 29, the company reported to regulators an FCCU opacity event, which flipped the differential from a 3.50-cent/gal discount into positive territory.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the price of Denver RBOB at a 35.85-cent/gal premium to NYMEX RBOB futures on Oct. 5, up 2.10 cents/gal from Oct. 2.
-- Janet Mcgurty

Platts proposes amending freight calculation for Oman, Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the freight calculation used in its daily Oman and Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
Under the proposed change, Platts would use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (DOOEW00), which was launched Aug. 3, in its calculation of the outright price of Oman and Murban delivered into North Asia at 4:30 pm Singapore time (0830 GMT).
The proposed change would better reflect the shipping economics for the two grades, with Murban primarily loading from Fujairah port and Oman from Mina Al Fahal port outside the Strait of Hormuz.
Currently, the freight calculation is based on the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (TDAAB00).

The freight calculation would continue to use the conversion factor for metric tons to barrels, as applied to the grades, in line with their prevailing qualities. The conversion factors are currently 7.32 for Oman and 7.62 for Murban.
In line with this change, Platts is also proposing to use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East worldscale freight assessment (DIOEW00) in the freight calculation for Murban in its Indonesian Minas, Cinta and Widuri crude market parity prices.
Under the current MPP methodology for Minas, Cinta and Widuri, Murban -- as one of five basket grades -- is normalized for delivery to Singapore, accounting for freight. This is currently calculated using the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Singapore worldscale freight assessment (PFAOD00).
The affected assessments appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Oilgram Price Report, LNG Daily, Platts OPR World Crude Table, Platts Global Alert/Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 1250, 1805, 2202, 2203, 2211, 2216, 2217, 2244, 2245, 2264, 2265, 2280, 2281, 2811 and 2812, LNG Alert pages 0050 and 0051, Platts Natural Gas Alert pages 1024 and 1069, and under the price database codes below:

Assessments Oman CFR North Asia Murban CFR North Asia Minas Singapore close Minas London close Cinta Singapore close Cinta London close Widuri Singapore close Widuri London close Minas FOB Indonesia $/

Platts proposes to amend rollover date of Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the rollover date of its Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close assessment to the first publication day of the month, from Dec. 1, 2026.
The proposed change, which follows Platts review of historical data sets, would align the ADB Strip at London close assessment

with the current rollover date of its ADB Strip at Asia close assessment.
Currently, this assessment, which reflects the forward value of Dated Brent two months ahead of the date of publication at the 4:30 pm London close, rolls over on the 9th of the month.
The assessments affected by this change appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Asia Pacific/Arab Gulf Marketscan, Oilgram Price Report, Platts Global Alert/ Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 2206, 2213, 2215, 2217, 2219, 2260, 2261, 2262, 2263, 2264, 2265, 2266, 2267, 2268, 2269, 2270, 2271, 2280, 2281, 2558, 2559, and under their price database codes as below:
